NQQR: scalable qutrit image representation
Authors: Mirna Rofail, Rasha Montaser, Ahmed Younes
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Ternary quantum image processing (TQIP) integrates qutrit-based quantum computing with image representation, offering a promising pathway for efficient image encoding models. This paper introduces Novel Qutrit Quantum Representation (NQQR) model, a scalable and optimized ternary model for representing grayscale and RGB images of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} in a ternary quantum system. The proposed model encodes pixel positions using the superposition of 2n qutrits and assigns grayscale or color intensity values using 6 qutrits, while an additional color qutrit and an ancilla qutrit are employed to reduce the complexity of multi-controlled N-qutrit gates, thereby lowering the overall quantum cost. Experimental results show that the NQQR achieves a time complexity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(n3^{2n})$$\end{document} and the optimized NQQR model significantly reduces quantum cost, with improvements up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$99.36\%$$\end{document} for grayscale images and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$99.33\%$$\end{document} for RGB images when compared to equivalent non-optimized qubit/qutrit-based encoding models. In addition, the performance of the proposed model is evaluated under quantum depolarizing noise using MSE and PSNR metrics computed between the original and reconstructed images under different noise levels. These results demonstrate the efficiency and scalability of the proposed NQQR model at the representation level and suggest its potential as a building block for future research in TQIP.Supplementary InformationThe online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1038/s41598-026-57304-9.

Introduction

Quantum computing expands the boundaries of computational power by leveraging the principles of quantum mechanics such as superposition and entanglement, enabling quantum systems to solve complex problems with a level of efficiency and parallelism that classical computers cannot achieve1–5. In addition to traditional binary quantum systems, ternary quantum systems use three-level quantum states (qutrits), which increase data density, reduce circuit complexity, and allow more efficient quantum algorithms 6–9. These advantages are particularly important in fields such as image processing10,11, cryptography 12,13 and machine learning14,15, where digital images are fundamental data units requiring efficient storage, manipulation, and retrieval.

Quantum image representation is an essential step in quantum image processing (QIP). While most existing representation models rely on binary qubits16–21, ternary quantum systems offer higher scalability and flexibility, providing more compact and efficient encoding of digital images22–25. In binary-based quantum systems, the Flexible Representation of Quantum Images (FRQI)16, the Novel Enhanced Quantum Representation (NEQR) 17, the Multi-Channel Quantum Representation (MCQI)18 and the Novel Quantum Representation of Color Digital Images (NCQI)19 have been widely used to represent grayscale and RGB images. Despite their effectiveness, binary models require a rapidly increasing number of qubits and more complex circuits as image dimensions grow. To address these scalability challenges, ternary (qutrit-based) models have been proposed as alternative encoding models. The Qutrit Representation of Grayscale Quantum Images (QTRQ) model22 introduced the first ternary model for representing grayscale images, followed by circuit-level optimizations that improved the image representation in the QTRQ model24. The Ternary Novel Colored Quantum Representation (TNCQR) model25 further extended ternary encoding by representing RGB images, lowering time complexity, and reducing quantum cost. However, these ternary models still suffer from several limitations: they are not fully native ternary models, as they are derived from binary models; the quantum cost remains high even after optimization; and as the image dimensions increase (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document}), circuit complexity grows rapidly, making practical implementation for real-world images challenging.

Despite the growing interest in QIP, several fundamental challenges remain, including issues related to measurement-induced information loss and unintended entanglement in certain representation models. These challenges limit the practical applicability of many existing approaches. In this work, we focus on improving the efficiency and scalability of ternary quantum image representation, which is a key component in QIP pipelines.

Accordingly, this paper proposes a Novel Quantum Qutrit Representation (NQQR) model. NQQR is a pure ternary quantum representation that enables compact and scalable encoding of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} grayscale and RGB images using qutrit-based quantum computation. By minimizing multi-qutrit gate usage, and applying optimization techniques, NQQR achieves lower quantum cost and improved scalability compared to relevant ternary and binary models such as QTRQ22, TNCQR25, FRQI16, MCQI18, NEQR17 and NCQI19. The main novelty of the proposed NQQR model lies in its native ternary formulation, which differentiates it from existing qubit-based and binary-derived ternary approaches. By directly exploiting qutrit systems, the model achieves higher information density, improved scalability, and reduced the reliance on complex multi-controlled operations. In addition to efficient image representation, the robustness of quantum image encoding under noise is a critical challenge in practical quantum systems. Therefore, this work also investigates the performance of the proposed model under quantum depolarizing noise using a statistical reconstruction framework based on Monte Carlo sampling. This approach enables effective reduction of quantum noise effects without relying on quantum error correction techniques.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows: Section Preliminaries provides background on quantum ternary logic. Section Related Works reviews existing ternary quantum image representation models. Section The Proposed NQQR Model for Grayscale Images introduces the proposed representation for grayscale images, while Section The Proposed NQQR Model for RGB Images extends the representation to RGB images in ternary quantum system. Section NQQR Optimization Method presents the optimization phases used to reduce the quantum cost of the constructed circuits for both grayscale and RGB images. Section Experimental Results evaluates the performance of the proposed model and compares it with relevant qubit/qutrit-based representation models. Section Performance Evaluation of NQQR Under Quantum Noise presents the performance of the proposed model under different levels of quantum depolarizing noise for both grayscale and RGB images. Finally, Section Conclusion concludes the paper and outlines directions for future work.

Preliminaries

This section provides a brief background on the fundamental ternary quantum gates that form the basis of the proposed image representation model. These gates operate on ternary quantum system, where each quantum state can exist in 3 distinct levels (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document},\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}), offering greater representational capacity compared to binary qubits. This section also presents definitions of the basic computational metrics adopted in this study, namely time complexity and quantum cost in ternary quantum systems.

Definition 1

The time complexity is the number of sequential layers of gates (non-parallelized operations) that must be executed to realize the circuit (circuit depth)17,25–28.

Definition 2

Quantum cost is a metric that assigns costs to gates according to the ternary quantum system architecture. Quantum cost evaluates complex (N-qutrit) gates by decomposing them into elementary ternary gates (one-qutrit and two-qutrit gates) and summing their costs24,25,28–32.

One-qutrit gates

In ternary quantum system there are 7 one-qutrit gates: the 6 ternary shift gates (Z gates)22–25,29,30,32,33 and the ternary Hadamard gate (H)22,24,25,34. Each one-qutrit gate operates on one qutrit to change the qutrit state. The Z gates are: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+0], [+1], [+2], [01], [02], [12]$$\end{document}. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+0]$$\end{document} gate is the identity gate (I). The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} gate is a single-shift gate that changes the ternary state by adding 1 mod 3. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} gate is a double-shift gate that changes the ternary state by adding 2 mod 3. The [01] gate is a self-single-shift gate that swaps the ternary state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document}. The [02] gate is a self-double-shift gate that swaps the ternary state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}. The [12] gate is a self-shift gate that swaps the ternary state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}22–25,29,30,32,33.

The ternary H gate is responsible for preparing the ternary superposition states. When applying the H gate on a ternary quantum state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \psi \right\rangle =\alpha \left| 0\right\rangle +\beta \left| 1\right\rangle +\gamma \left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} it produces the general form of the ternary superposition states, as shown in Eq (1)22,24,25,34,1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{ H\left| \psi \right\rangle =\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(\alpha +\beta +\gamma )\left| 0\right\rangle +\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(\alpha +\beta e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}+\gamma e^{\frac{4\pi i}{3}})\left| 1\right\rangle +\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(\alpha +\beta e^{\frac{4\pi i}{3}}+\gamma e^{\frac{8\pi i}{3}})\left| 2\right\rangle .} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}To maintain reversibility in ternary quantum circuits, each one-qutrit gate must have an inverse gate to reverse its function. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} are mutual inverses, which means that they inverse each other’s function. The gates [01], [02], [12], and H are self-inverse, which mean that applying the same gate twice results in the identity function (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+0]$$\end{document})24,30,32. Figure 1 shows the symbolic notation for Z and H gates as quantum circuits, each with quantum cost equals 1 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC=1$$\end{document})24,29–32. Table 1 presents the truth table for the 7 one-qutrit gates: the 6 ternary Z gates and the ternary H gate.Fig. 1The symbolic notation of one-qutrit gates as a quantum circuit: (a) The notation of any Z gate. \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(\left| 0\right\rangle +e^{\frac{4\pi i}{3}}\left| 1\right\rangle +e^{\frac{8\pi i}{3}}\left| 2\right\rangle )$$\end{document}

The Truth table for the 7 one-qutrit gates30,32,34.

Two-qutrit M-S gates

The two-qutrit gates proposed by Muthukrishnan and Stroud35 operate on 2 qutrits: a control qutrit and a target qutrit. The Z gate on the target qutrit is applied when the control qutrit satisfies the conditional state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| a\right\rangle$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a \in \{0,1,2\}$$\end{document} as shown in Figure 2a. The QC for two-qutrit gates is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2k+1$$\end{document}, where k is the number of controls not in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}22,24,25,29–33,36.

Figure 2b shows the decomposition of generalized two-qutrit gate when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| a\right\rangle \ne \left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} into 3 gates: 2 one-qutrit gates and 1 two-qutrit gate with control qutrit in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}. In this figure, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+(2-a)]$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+(1+a])$$\end{document} represent the gates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+((2-a)\,mod\,3)]$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+((1+a)\,mod\,3)]$$\end{document}, respectively, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in \{\left| 0\right\rangle ,\left| 1\right\rangle ,\left| 2\right\rangle \}$$\end{document}.Fig. 2Symbolic notations for the two-qutrit gates as a quantum circuit: (a) The notation \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| x\right\rangle$$\end{document} is in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} (c) The decomposition of generalized two-qutrit gate.

Generalized N-qutrit M-S gate

The generalized N-qutrit M-S gate operates on N qutrits. The Z gate on the target qutrit is activated when all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-1$$\end{document} control qutrits are in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}, as shown in Figure 3a, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC=4N-7$$\end{document}22,25, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \ge 2$$\end{document} qutrits.

To decompose the N-qutrit gate, add \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-2$$\end{document} ancilla qutrits in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}, then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-1$$\end{document} control qutrits are decomposed into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2N-4$$\end{document} two-qutrit gates with a target \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} gate, followed by 1 two-qutrit gate with a target Z gate, and finally \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2N-4$$\end{document} two-qutrit gates with a target \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} gate. Figure 3b illustrates the decomposition of the N-qutrit M-S gate22,29,31.Fig. 3Symbolic notation for the N-qutrit gate as a quantum circuit: (a) The default notation of N-qutrit gate with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N - 1$$\end{document} control qutrits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| x_{_j}\right\rangle$$\end{document} all in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in \{1,2,3,...,N-1\}$$\end{document} (b) The decomposition of N-qutrit gate.

While qutrit controls can operate in any state: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document}, or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}, an N-qutrit gate can operate with any control state, as shown in Fig. 4a. Figure 4b illustrates the decomposition of a generalized N-qutrit gate. The QC for this gate is calculated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC = 4N + 2k - 7$$\end{document}22,25, where N is the number of qutrits used in the gate, and k is the number of controls not in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}.Fig. 4Symbolic notation for the generalized N-qutrit gate as a quantum circuit: (a) The generalized N-qutrit gate with control qutrits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| x_{_j}\right\rangle$$\end{document} function according to state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| a_{_j}\right\rangle \in \{\left| 0\right\rangle , \left| 1\right\rangle ,\left| 2\right\rangle \}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in \{1, 2,3,...,N-1\}$$\end{document} (b) The decomposition of generalized N-qutrit gate.

Related works

This section presents recent relevant models for representing digital images in ternary quantum system such as: the QTRQ22 and TNCQR models25.

QTRQ representation model

The QTRQ model22 is designed to encode grayscale images into a ternary quantum system. It employs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n + q$$\end{document} qutrits to represent a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} grayscale image, where the grayscale value in range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0-255]$$\end{document} is encoded using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q = 6$$\end{document} qutrits per pixel, instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q = 8$$\end{document} qubits used in binary-based models. The mathematical expression for representing a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} grayscale quantum image using the QTRQ model is expressed as follows,2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {QTRQ}}=\dfrac{1}{3^n}\sum _{Y=0}^{3^n-1}\sum _{X=0}^{3^n-1}\left| f(Y,X)\right\rangle \left| YX\right\rangle =\dfrac{1}{3^n}\sum _{Y=0}^{3^n-1}\sum _{X=0}^{3^n-1}\bigotimes _{l=0}^{q-1}\left| C^{^{\,l}}_{_{YX}}\right\rangle \left| YX\right\rangle , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| f(Y,X)\right\rangle$$\end{document} denotes the grayscale value of the pixel located at position (Y, X), encoded as a ternary sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{0}_{YX} C^{1}_{YX} \ldots C^{q-1}_{YX}$$\end{document}, where each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| C^{^{\,l}}_{_{YX}}\right\rangle \in {\left| 0\right\rangle , \left| 1\right\rangle , \left| 2\right\rangle }$$\end{document}. Figure 5 presents a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3 \times 3$$\end{document} grayscale image and its corresponding quantum image state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {QTRQ}}$$\end{document}, where each pixel is indexed by its (Y, X) position and assigned a grayscale value in the range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0-255]$$\end{document}. The quantum circuit constructed using the QTRQ model records a total quantum cost of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC = 203$$\end{document}22. Additional optimization techniques described in 22 further reduce the quantum cost to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC = 107$$\end{document}.Fig. 5A \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\times 3$$\end{document} grayscale image with its corresponding quantum image state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {QTRQ}}$$\end{document} represented using QTRQ model.

A \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\times 3$$\end{document} grayscale image with its corresponding quantum image state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {QTRQ}}$$\end{document} represented using QTRQ model.

The QTRQ model can be extended to represent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} RGB digital images by adding one additional qutrit to encode the 3 color channels25. In this extension, the Red, Green, and Blue channels are represented by the 3 qutrit states (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}) through the application of the ternary Hadamard (H) gate on the color qutrit. Therefore, the extended model requires \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n + q + 1$$\end{document} qutrits to store a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} RGB image with a color range of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0-255]$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q = 6$$\end{document} qutrits are used to represent the color value of each channel per pixel. Figure6 presents a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\times 3$$\end{document} RGB image and the corresponding quantum image state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {QTRQ}}$$\end{document} for the RGB image. The time complexity for preparing a quantum image using the QTRQ model is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(qn3^{2n})$$\end{document}.Fig. 6A \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\times 3$$\end{document} RGB image with its corresponding quantum image state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {QTRQ}}$$\end{document} represented using QTRQ model25.

A \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\times 3$$\end{document} RGB image with its corresponding quantum image state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {QTRQ}}$$\end{document} represented using QTRQ model25.

Although the QTRQ model provides a compact way to represent quantum images using qutrits instead of qubits, it has several limitations. First, the model is not a true ternary representation but a conversion from the NEQR binary model. Second, it uses a limited set of generalized N-qutrit gates with 2n or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n+1$$\end{document} controls, which increases the circuit depth and results in a high time complexity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(q n 3^{2n})$$\end{document}. Third, the quantum cost is still high even for small images. Finally, as the image size grows to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document}, the number of qutrits and the circuit complexity increase quickly, making it difficult to scale to larger or real-world images.

TNCQR representation model

The TNCQR model is designed to encode \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} RGB digital images with time complexity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(n3^{2n})$$\end{document} and a lower quantum cost compared to the QTRQ model25. In the TNCQR model, 7 qutrits are used to encode the color intensity of each pixel. Therefore, the model requires \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n + q + 2$$\end{document} qutrits to store a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} RGB image, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q = 6$$\end{document} qutrits are used to represent the color range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0-255]$$\end{document} for each pixel. An ancilla qutrit is used to load the position of each pixel and assign its value within the range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0-255]$$\end{document} using up to 5 two-qutrit gates. The inclusion of this ancilla qutrit reduces the overall circuit cost by reducing the number of complex N-qutrit gates required, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \ge 3$$\end{document}. The representative expression for storing a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} RGB quantum image is given in Eq (3), and the corresponding quantum image state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {TNCQR}}$$\end{document} for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3 \times 3$$\end{document} RGB image in Figure 6 is shown in Eq (4). The constructed quantum circuit for this image using the TNCQR model has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC = 267$$\end{document}. An optimization algorithm has been applied to reduce the quantum cost to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC = 170$$\end{document}25.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {TNCQR}}&=\dfrac{1}{3^n\sqrt{3}}\sum _{Y=0}^{3^n-1}\sum _{X=0}^{3^n-1}\left| f(Y,X)\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle _{\text {aux}}\left| YX\right\rangle \\&=\dfrac{1}{3^n\sqrt{3}}\sum _{Y=0}^{3^n-1}\sum _{X=0}^{3^n-1}\Big (\left| R_{YX}\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle +\left| G_{YX}\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle +\left| B_{YX}\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle \Big )\left| 0\right\rangle _{\text {aux}}\left| YX\right\rangle . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \left| Img\right\rangle _{_\text {TNCQR}}=&\frac{1}{3\sqrt{3}}&\Bigl ( (\left| 100021\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 00\right\rangle \\ & + & (\left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 100021\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 01\right\rangle \\ & + & (\left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 100021\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 02\right\rangle \\ & + & (\left| 100110\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 100021\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 10\right\rangle \\ & + & (\left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 100110\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 100021\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 11\right\rangle \\ & + & (\left| 100110\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 100021\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 12\right\rangle \\ & + & (\left| 100021\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 021002\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 20\right\rangle \\ & + & (\left| 021002\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 000000\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 021002\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 21\right\rangle \\ & + & (\left| 100110\right\rangle \left| 0\right\rangle+ & \left| 022112\right\rangle \left| 1\right\rangle+ & \left| 100021\right\rangle \left| 2\right\rangle )&\quad \left| 0\right\rangle _{aux}\left| 22\right\rangle\Bigr ) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The TNCQR model is also applicable to grayscale images. Implementing the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3 \times 3$$\end{document} grayscale image shown in Figure 5, results in a quantum circuit with initial quantum cost \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC = 149$$\end{document}, which is reduced to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QC = 85$$\end{document} using the optimization algorithm, matching the quantum cost reported in24.

Although the TNCQR model is better than the QTRQ model in terms of quantum cost and time complexity, it still has some limitations. First, the model is not fully native ternary representation but is derived from the ENEQR binary model. Second, the quantum cost remains relatively high even after optimization. To overcome these limitations, this paper proposes the NQQR model, a novel ternary representation that exploits qutrit-based computation to provide a more compact and resource-efficient encoding of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} grayscale and RGB images in quantum systems.

The proposed NQQR model for grayscale images

This section introduces the proposed Novel Qutrit Quantum Representation model (NQQR) for representing grayscale images in ternary quantum system. To validate the proposed representation at the circuit level, all quantum circuits are implemented and evaluated using Google Cirq 37 for ternary quantum systems.

NQQR image representation model

The NQQR model represents a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n \times 3^n$$\end{document} grayscale image in ternary quantum system, as shown in the general ternary quantum circuit in Figure 7. The representation process consists of the following G-Steps, where each ”G-Step” refers to a specific stage in encoding the grayscale image using the proposed NQQR model.Fig. 7The general ternary target gate to one of the q qutrits.G-Step V: A generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (column position X) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} G-Step I initializes the grayscale image as the quantum state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \Psi _0\right\rangle$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q+1+2n$$\end{document} qutrits all initialized to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}, as shown in Eq (5). then applied on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \begin{document}$$3^{2n}$$\end{document} pixels. This operator applies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{\otimes 2n}$$\end{document} gates on the 2n position qutrits, creating a superposition of all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^{2n}$$\end{document} pixel positions. This operator operates iteratively on each row of pixels in the image, starting from Row\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{_0}$$\end{document} till Row\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{_{3^n-1}}$$\end{document}. For each Row\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{_Y}$$\end{document}, the gray-setting quantum operation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varpi _{_{YX}}$$\end{document} assigns the grayscale value for all pixels in that row, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| YX\right\rangle$$\end{document} represents the pixel currently being processed and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| vu\right\rangle$$\end{document} represents the other pixels in the image that are not currently being updated. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \Bigr )\Re _{_{Y}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The sub-operation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} pixel (Y, X) as n control qutrits to update the state of the ancilla qutrit from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} (G-Step II). The sub-operation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varsigma _{_{X}}$$\end{document} is an N-qutrit gate that uses the column position X of the pixel (Y, X) as n control qutrits to update the state of the ancilla qutrit from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} (G-Step III). The sub-operation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{_{Y X}}$$\end{document} assigns the grayscale value in ternary representation over the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q=6$$\end{document} qutrits to the pixel (Y, X) in range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0 - 255]$$\end{document} (G-Step IV). Therefore, the sub-operation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{_{Y X}}$$\end{document} consists of q unitary operations as follows,10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{_{Y X}}=\bigotimes ^{^{q-1}}_{_{l=0}}\Omega _{_{Y X}}^{^{l}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{_{Y X}}^{^{l}}$$\end{document} unitary operator is a 1 two-qutrit M-S gate that is applied to 1 of the q qutrits to change its state from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} according to the pixel’s grayscale value as follows,11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{_{Y X}}^{^{l}}:\left| 0\right\rangle \rightarrow \left| 0\oplus C^{^{^l}}_{_{Y X). After assigning the grayscale value using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{_{Y X}}$$\end{document}, another \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varsigma _{_{X}}$$\end{document} gate is used to update the state of the ancilla qutrit from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} to prepare the representation of the next pixel in the same row (G-Step V). Finally, the ancilla qutrit state is returned to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document} by applying the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Re _{_{Y}}$$\end{document} gate, preparing it for the representation of the next row of pixels in the image (G-Step VI).

The general ternary quantum circuit for representing a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n\times 3^n$$\end{document} grayscale image using the NQQR model.

G-Step I: The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n+q+1$$\end{document} qutrits are initialized to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{\otimes {2n}}$$\end{document} gates are applied on the 2n position qutrits.

G-Step I: The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n+q+1$$\end{document} qutrits are initialized to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{\otimes {2n}}$$\end{document} gates are applied on the 2n position qutrits.

G-Step II: A generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (row position Y) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} target gate is used to update the ancilla qutrit from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} (Enable row Y).

G-Step II: A generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (row position Y) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} target gate is used to update the ancilla qutrit from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} (Enable row Y).

G-Step III: Another generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (column position X) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} target gate is used to update the ancilla qutrit from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} (Enable column X).

G-Step III: Another generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (column position X) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} target gate is used to update the ancilla qutrit from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} (Enable column X).

G-Step IV: The grayscale value of the pixel (Y, X), in range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0-255]$$\end{document}, is assigned using up to 5 two-qutrit gates, where each gate uses the ancilla qutrit in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} as the control qutrit and applies a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} target gate to one of the q qutrits.

G-Step IV: The grayscale value of the pixel (Y, X), in range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0-255]$$\end{document}, is assigned using up to 5 two-qutrit gates, where each gate uses the ancilla qutrit in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} as the control qutrit and applies a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+1]$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} target gate to one of the q qutrits.

G-Step V: A generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (column position X) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} target gate is used to reverse the effect of G-Step III, returning the ancilla state from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document}, preparing the representation of the next pixel in the same row Y (Disable column X).

G-Step V: A generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (column position X) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} target gate is used to reverse the effect of G-Step III, returning the ancilla state from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document}, preparing the representation of the next pixel in the same row Y (Disable column X).

Repeat G-Steps III−V to assign the grayscale value for all pixels in the same row\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textit{Y}$$\end{document}.

Repeat G-Steps III−V to assign the grayscale value for all pixels in the same row\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textit{Y}$$\end{document}.

G-Step VI: A generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (row position Y) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} target gate is used to reverse the effect of Step II, returning the ancilla state from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}, preparing the representation of the next row of pixels in the image (Disable row Y).

G-Step VI: A generalized N-qutrit gate with n control qutrits (row position Y) and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[+2]$$\end{document} target gate is used to reverse the effect of Step II, returning the ancilla state from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 1\right\rangle$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 0\right\rangle$$\end{document}, preparing the representation of the next row of pixels in the image (Disable row Y).

The G-Steps II−VI are repeated to represent all pixels in the image.

The G-Steps II−VI are repeated to represent all pixels in the image.

Each of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varpi _{_{YX}}$$\end{document} sub-operations is implemented in the ternary quantum circuit for the NQQR model as follows: the sub-operations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Re _{_{Y}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varsigma _{_{X}}$$\end{document} are generalized N-qutrit M-S gates, each with n control and 1 target qutrits, where n determines the size of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^n\times 3^n$$\end{document} image. The sub-operation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{_{Y X}}$$\end{document} assigns the grayscale value for 1 pixel over the q qutrits and is implemented using up to 5 two-qutrit M-S gates, each with a control qutrit set to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| 2\right\rangle$$\end{document}, since representing values from 0 to 255 in ternary requires modifying up to 5 ternary digits.
